---
name: no-sophistry-in-lesson-copy
description: "Lesson copy must be plain, natural and personable; define the idea first (contrapositive = logical equivalent), no \"promise / depends on / nothing to stand on\" talk; log every new correction."
metadata:
  node_type: memory
  type: feedback
  originSessionId: 6f1c3429-949a-4b8a-b0c8-a960c83f510f
  modified: 2026-10-12T02:07:52.687Z
---

When writing or editing LSAT lesson copy, say what the thing is in plain words first, then show it. Cut sophistry: no "a rule makes a promise", no "the left side depends on the right", no "nothing to stand on", no vague "the other one". Keep it natural, intuitive, personable, in his teaching voice.

**Why:** On 2026-10-11 Joshua rejected the first contrapositive lesson as "terribly done", then rejected the rewrite for "a lot of sophistry". He wants the contrapositive taught as the logical equivalent: the same rule said a different way, interchangeable, equally valid. His own session transcripts say exactly that.

**How to apply:**
- Read `wiki/contrapositive-teaching.md` in the vault before any contrapositive work. It holds his own wording and the mistakes he warns about.
- Order for a concept lesson: what it is, see it work, how to do it, what it is not, the steps in short, why it matters.
- Every time he corrects wording, add it to `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md` the same turn, with the reason. Read that file before writing new notes. This is how the same mistake is not repeated.
- Title lessons by topic ("Contrapositive"). Card fronts ask a specific question and the back gives the answer first. "Correct", not "Right".
- Related: [[no-em-dashes-no-filler-in-joshuas-writing]], [[lsat-teaching-voice-standard]], [[no-internal-labels-in-student-lessons]].
- Never say the contrapositive is "the one other thing" a rule tells you (his 2026-10-11 correction). Say equivalent and interchangeable: know one, you know the other.
- Do not use the raffle/bicycle as the example in new contrapositive or arrow material (the contrapositive page is raffle-free as of 2026-10-12; earlier reference steps, opening lessons and foundations source still use it). Use a fresh everyday example (current contrapositive note: elevator moving, doors closed).
- Contrapositive lesson: no thought experiment or guess card (it confused him). Follow the source HTML explanation directly in close language, definition first, equivalence stated, "left side / right side of the arrow". Never mention Logic Games. Never reuse the source coffee or breathing/air examples.
- Rules say "the power MUST be on". Avoid the word "foreign". Voice = his Intro to LR bootcamp: plain claim, short stacked landings, "Here's the thing", dry wit. A cheeky exclamation on the one key word is OK in the contrapositive lesson, with a one-time punch-in and glow; motion must carry the reasoning and respect reduced motion.
- NEVER show negation as a squiggle (~) beside an idea, anywhere, including answer choices and the reference practice. Use the word "not" or a diagonal strike from the idea's top-left to bottom-right corner (component Neg / Notation in ContrapositiveVisuals.tsx; the ~ is gone from the conditionals module as of 2026-10-12). Write the contrapositive as a plain sentence early.
- Anything that is wrong must LOOK wrong: red card, X badge, the word "Wrong" (reverse and inverse cards). Look-alike wrong answers and the right one never share a table. No repeated content; high-end, sleek visuals for anything interactive.
---

## General standards confirmed by repeated corrections

- Compare the topic against internal corpus sources before drafting. Preserve source provenance and distinguish a source claim from Joshua's decision. Ingest new clips through the ingest skill before relying on them. Never infer a video transcript from its title.
- Explain the concept in plain language, then develop it through a concrete familiar example. Introduce notation after meaning. A short definition alone is not a lesson. Avoid recursive definitions and tongue twisters.
- Break explanations into readable sections with clear visual hierarchy, larger text, generous spacing, and short paragraphs. Inspect the actual rendered page, including the docked browser width and mobile, rather than treating JSX inspection as visual approval.
- Bring meaningful interaction early, after enough teaching to attempt it. Feedback explains the reasoning and distinguishes given facts from conclusions of a rule. Do not merely cycle reveal buttons and call that practice.
- Use color, emphasis, and motion to direct attention to the entire relevant idea. Preserve complete conditions, restrictions, timing, modality, and the difference between a rule and a fact.
- Preserve existing teaching media while revising. Check that videos still exist and play in their intended lesson. New media must earn its place in the teaching flow.

28	## Necessary-condition lesson: explicitly approved wording
29	
30	Joshua supplied and approved this copy on 2026-10-11:
31	
32	> **A necessary condition is a requirement. It has to be met, but meeting it might not be enough.**
33	> For example, you have to be a student to be enrolled in law school. That makes being a student **necessary for law-school enrollment**.
34	> But being a student doesn’t mean you’re enrolled in law school. You could be studying history instead.
35	> **Being a student is required. It doesn’t establish enrollment on its own.**
36	
37	Keep “might not”: a condition may be both necessary and sufficient. Do not replace this with “something another condition can’t be true without.”
38	
39	Explicit page corrections:
40	- Emphasize the complete consequent, “then you are a student,” and identify “you are a student” as the condition. Do not teach isolated “then” as a standalone necessary-condition indicator.
41	- Law student / history student / not a student should be vertical case tabs. Keep keyboard navigation, visible selected state, and mobile stacking. This is a decision for this case comparison, not a mandate for every interaction.
42	- Remove “Replay emphasis”; use a gentle continuous pulse for this page, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. This does not authorize constant pulsing on every page.
43	- Make clear which facts are given and which follow. If a history case says not enrolled, make it explicitly given rather than inferring it from studying history.
44	
45	Content preservation checks for the conditional sequence:
46	- Necessary examples retain only, only if, only when,...

// 7-module linear bootcamp definition.
//
// Each module has 3 parts: Note, Quiz, Drill. Lessons and drills are tagged
// to modules via the maps below (kept here rather than in-line on each item
// so the verbatim source comments in data/drills.ts and data/lessons.ts stay
// intact). The lookup helpers expose moduleId on a per-item basis.

export type ModuleId = "m1" | "m2" | "m3" | "m4" | "m5" | "m6" | "m7";

export type ModulePart = "note" | "quiz" | "drill";

export type QuizSource =
  | "whyHardItems"
  | "termQuizItems"
  | "translatorItems"
  | "matchingItems"
  | "methodQuizItems"
  | "trapItems"
  | "abstractChoices";

export interface ModuleRecord {
  id: ModuleId;
  number: number;
  name: string;
  subtitle: string;
  noteLessonId: string;
  quizSource: QuizSource;
  quizPassThreshold: number;
  drillIds: string[];
}

export const MODULES: ModuleRecord[] = [
  {
    id: "m1",
    number: 1,
    name: "Why Abstract Is Hard",
    subtitle: "Feel the difficulty before learning the fix.",
    noteLessonId: "why_hard",
    quizSource: "whyHardItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_1", "drill_2"],
  },
  {
    id: "m2",
    number: 2,
    name: "LSAT Words You Must Know",
    subtitle: "Seven terms with LSAT-specific meanings.",
    noteLessonId: "words_to_know",
    quizSource: "termQuizItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_3", "drill_4"],
  },
  {
    id: "m3",
    number: 3,
    name: "Stimulus + Keyword Extraction",
    subtitle: "The core method. Find the load-bearing words.",
    noteLessonId: "deabstractifying_method",
    quizSource: "translatorItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_5", "drill_6", "drill_7", "drill_8", "drill_9"],
  },
  {
    id: "m4",
    number: 4,
    name: "Match To The Stimulus",
    subtitle: "Test each part of the answer choice against the stimulus.",
    noteLessonId: "art_of_matching",
    quizSource: "matchingItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_10", "drill_11", "drill_12", "drill_13", "drill_14"],
  },
  {
    id: "m5",
    number: 5,
    name: "Spot The Method",
    subtitle: "Seventeen method-of-reasoning patterns.",
    noteLessonId: "master_keys",
    quizSource: "methodQuizItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_15", "drill_16"],
  },
  {
    id: "m6",
    number: 6,
    name: "Catch The Traps",
    subtitle: "One-word swaps, wrong roles, structural mismatches.",
    noteLessonId: "five_bucket_indicators",
    quizSource: "trapItems",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_17", "drill_18"],
  },
  {
    id: "m7",
    number: 7,
    name: "Put It Together",
    subtitle: "The whole workflow. The full 18-item gauntlet.",
    noteLessonId: "abstract_15",
    quizSource: "abstractChoices",
    quizPassThreshold: 0.6,
    drillIds: ["drill_19", "drill_20"],
  },
];

export function getModule(id: ModuleId): ModuleRecord | undefined {
  return MODULES.find((m) => m.id === id);
}

const DRILL_MODULE_MAP: Record<string, ModuleId> = MODULES.reduce(
  (acc, m) => {
    for (const drillId of m.drillIds) acc[drillId] = m.id;
    return acc;
  },
  {} as Record<string, ModuleId>,
);

const LESSON_MODULE_MAP: Record<string, ModuleId> = MODULES.reduce(
  (acc, m) => {
    acc[m.noteLessonId] = m.id;
    return acc;
  },
  {} as Record<string, ModuleId>,
);

export function moduleIdForDrill(drillId: string): ModuleId | undefined {
  return DRILL_MODULE_MAP[drillId];
}

export function moduleIdForLesson(lessonId: string): ModuleId | undefined {
  return LESSON_MODULE_MAP[lessonId];
}
import { Lesson } from "../types";

// 8 lessons for the Abstract bootcamp.
//
// Order intentional per spec: foundation -> tooling -> framework -> practice.
// `abstract_15` retains its stage gate (now 18 items, name preserved for
// id-stability across Supabase rows that may already reference it).
//
// The three new lessons (master_keys, five_bucket_indicators, art_of_matching)
// are sourced from _source...

2026-10-12
25-- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student's own history. Praise is a specific observation, not "great job."
26-- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard ("expect this to be exhausting and very challenging"). Never be blunt about a student's shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.
27:- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: "partner with you on your LSAT journey," "getting set up," "foundational." The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.
28-- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.
29-- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype ("unlock," "crush," "level up," "game-changer"; "crush" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers ("Sorry for such a long delay!"). He is not exclamation-averse.
30-- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what's ahead.
31-- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.
32-
33-## Openings, closings, and signature phrases
34-
35-The mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.

interface CalloutProps { title: string; children: React.ReactNode; variant?: 'default' | 'plain'; }
const Callout = ({ title, children, variant = 'default' }: CalloutProps) => (
  <div className={cn("p-6 md:p-7 border-2 border-brutal-ink mb-6", variant === 'default' ? "bg-brutal-yellow" : "bg-white")}>
    <h4 className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-3 text-brutal-ink">{title}</h4>
    <div className="text-brutal-ink">{children}</div>
  </div>
);

const Checklist = ({ items }: { items: string[] }) => (
  <div className="bg-brutal-cyan border-4 border-brutal-ink p-8 rounded-xl my-8 brutal-shadow">
    <h4 className="text-xs font-black text-brutal-ink uppercase tracking-widest mb-6">Check the match</h4>
    <div className="space-y-4">
      {items.map((item, i) => (
        <div key={i} className="flex items-center gap-4 text-brutal-ink font-bold">
          <div className="w-6 h-6 border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-white flex-shrink-0" />
          {item}
        </div>
      ))}
    </div>
  </div>
);

const RuleCard = ({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) => (
  <div className="bg-white border-4 border-brutal-ink p-8 rounded-xl my-10 brutal-shadow-lg relative overflow-hidden">
    <div className="absolute top-0 right-0 w-24 h-8 bg-brutal-pink border-b-4 border-l-4 border-brutal-ink -mr-2 -mt-2"></div>
    <h4 className="text-xs font-black text-brutal-ink uppercase tracking-widest mb-4">TAKEAWAY</h4>
    <p className="text-2xl text-brutal-ink font-black leading-tight tracking-tight uppercase">{children}</p>
  </div>
);

const MethodStep = ({ step, title, explanation, action }: { step: number; title: string; explanation: string; action: string }) => (
  <div className="bg-white p-6 rounded-xl border-4 border-brutal-ink mb-6 brutal-shadow">
    <div className="text-brutal-ink font-black text-xl mb-2 uppercase tracking-tighter italic">Step {step}: {title}</div>
    <p className="text-brutal-ink font-bold mb-4 leading-tight">{explanation}</p>
    <div className="bg-brutal-green border-2 border-brutal-ink p-5 rounded-lg text-sm text-brutal-ink font-bold brutal-shadow-sm">
      <span className="font-black block mb-2 uppercase tracking-widest text-xs">What to do:</span>
      {action}
    </div>
  </div>
);

const DecodePracticeCard = () => {
  const [step, setStep] = useState(0);

  return (
    <div className="space-y-8">
      <div className="bg-white border-4 border-brutal-ink p-8 brutal-shadow-sm">
        <div className="flex justify-between items-center mb-6">
          <span className="text-brutal-ink font-black text-xs uppercase tracking-widest">1. Extract the keywords</span>
          {step === 0 && (
            <button 
              onClick={() => setStep(1)} 
              className="px-6 py-2 bg-brutal-cyan border-2 border-brutal-ink text-brutal-ink text-xs font-black hover:bg-brutal-cyan/80 transition-all uppercase brutal-shadow-sm"
            >
              Reveal Keywords
            </button>
          )}
        </div>
        {step >= 1 && (

## assistant

Saving the session to [brainstorms/2026-10-12-abstract-bootcamp-fix.md](brainstorms/2026-10-12-abstract-bootcamp-fix.md).

What I found: Abstract has 7 modules (Why Abstract Is Hard → Words You Must Know → Keyword Extraction → Match to the Stimulus → Spot the Method → Catch the Traps → Put It Together), 20 drills, flashcards, a methods library and a timed final set. It uses its own yellow neo-brutalist look. Most lesson "practice" is Reveal-button cycling, which the Contrapositive standard rejects. It's live. I'm checking separately whether any students have saved progress in it.

**Round 1**

**Q1 - What "fix" means**: Which of these do you mean?
(a) Rebuild every lesson note to the Contrapositive standard: plain definition first, one example plus one fresh one, a demo the student drives, "what it is not", a Pop Quiz. Quizzes and drills keep their questions but get the new feedback style.
(b) Copy and wording only, keeping the current pages and interactions.
(c) A full redesign, including whether all seven modules should stay.

Recommended: (a). The structure is reasonable. The weak parts are the teaching and the reveal-button practice.

---

**Q2 - Look**: Should Abstract keep its yellow neo-brutalist style, or move to the shared LSAT U lesson look used by Intro to LR and Conditionals?

Recommended: Move to the shared look. Uppercase italic body text with heavy borders is hard to read in long notes, and students shouldn't feel like they've switched products between bootcamps.

---

**Q3 - First page to rebuild**: The usual process is to rebuild one page, get your approval, then roll the pattern out. Which page goes first?

Recommended: Module 3, Keyword Extraction. It teaches the core method that every later module uses, so locking its wording and demo first sets the pattern for the rest.

---

**Q4 - Name**: The Library lists it as just "Abstract". Keep that, or rename it to something like "Abstract Answer Choices"?

Recommended: "Abstract Answer Choices". "Abstract" alone doesn't tell a student what they'll practice, and you recently rejected titles for the same reason.

---

**Q5 - Sources**: Should the rebuild draw on `corpus/Curriculum/Abstract` (parsing worksheets, answer keys, the Translation and Cluster Sentences quiz), the module's existing source notes, or something else you have in mind, such as your session transcripts or an LSAT Lab or Demon lesson?

Recommended: Corpus plus the existing source notes, with fresh examples written for the page, as we did for Conditionals.

export type ConfidenceLevel = 'Guessing' | 'Unsure' | 'Pretty sure' | 'Confident';

export type ViewState = 'dashboard' | 'drill' | 'completion_report' | 'lesson' | 'flashcards' | 'methods_library' | 'mixed_practice' | 'timed_final_set' | 'timed_set' | 'module';

export type ModulePartName = 'note' | 'quiz' | 'drill';

export type MasteryLevel = 'none' | 'practicing' | 'solid' | 'mastered';

export interface ModuleProgressRow {
  moduleId: string;
  noteComplete: boolean;
  quizScore: number;
  quizTotal: number;
  quizStreakHigh: number;
  drillComplete: boolean;
  drillCorrect?: number;
  drillTotal?: number;
  aced: boolean;
  masteryLevel: MasteryLevel;
  completedAt?: string;
}

export type DrillType = 
  | 'keyword_extraction'
  | 'term_definition'
  | 'translation'
  | 'matching'
  | 'one_word_trap'
  | 'role'
  | 'method'
  | 'flaw'
  | 'mixed';

export interface CardReview {
  cardId: string;
  status: 'unseen' | 'seen' | 'reviewing' | 'mastered';
  lastReviewedAt?: string;
  reviewCount: number;
  // SRS bookkeeping. Optional so legacy localStorage records stay readable;
  // missing fields imply a brand-new card scheduled to be due immediately.
  rating?: 'again' | 'hard' | 'good' | 'easy';
  intervalDays?: number;
  dueAtIso?: string;
}

export interface Flashcard {
  id: string;
  question: string;

}

export interface MethodPattern {

  name: string;

  example: {
    stimulus: string;
    explanation: string;
  };
}

// DrillAttempt: per-attempt record retained client-side for the Drill view

}

}

}

import LessonReader from "./LessonReader";

// LessonBody: shared lesson body renderer for ModuleNote.
//
// Strips the standalone reader chrome (nav, hero title, outer card border,
// completion CTA) and applies the cased-body CSS scope so prose reads in
// regular weight at 1.7 line-height instead of the standalone view's
// uppercase italic. The hosting ModuleNote owns the scrollable container,
// the read-this button, and the quiz CTA.

export default function LessonBody({ lessonId }: { lessonId: string }) {
  return (
    <LessonReader
      lockedLessonId={lessonId}
      hideNav
      hideComplete
      hideTitle
      hideCard
      casedBody
    />
  );
}

# LSAT Logical Reasoning: Level 1 Answer Parsing & Mapping

**Student Name:** ________________ **Date:** ________________

## Instructions

To master Method of Reasoning and Flaw questions, you must be able to "de-stractify" the answer choices. Do not just read these phrases; map them.

- **The Conclusion** is the Destination (where the argument is going).
- **The Evidence** is the "Method of Transportation" (how it gets there).

## Part I: The Vocabulary of Logic

*Before mapping structure, you must define the terms accurately. In the context of the LSAT, define the strict logical meaning of the following terms.*

| Term | LSAT Definition & Requirement |
|---|---|
| **1. "Explanation"** | *Hint: It does not mean "elaborating" or "providing detail."* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |
| **2. "Defend"** | *Hint: Stating a motivation is not a defense.* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |
| **3. "Aesthetic Principle"** | *Hint: It is not just a specific opinion about one painting.* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |
| **4. "Supported Premise"** | *Hint: It functions as both a roof and a floor.* **Also known as:** _________________ |

## Part II: Structural Mapping (X vs. Y)

*For the following abstract answer choices, identifying the variable is not enough. You must identify the **Indicator Word** that tells you the direction of the logic.*

### Task

1. **Circle** the Indicator Word (e.g., "because," "from," "by").
2. Identify which variable represents the **Conclusion (C)** and which represents the **Evidence (E)**.

| Abstract Phrase | Indicator Word | Conclusion | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Example: infers that X on the grounds that Y* | *on the grounds that* | **X** | **Y** |
| 1. doing **X** by doing **Y** | | | |
| 2. using **X** to support **Y** | | | |
| 3. concludes, from the claim that **X**, that **Y** | | | |
| 4. concludes **X** on the basis of **Y** | | | |
| 5. infers, on the grounds that **X**, that **Y** | | | |
| 6. takes for granted that **Y** because **X** | | | |
| 7. **Y** is true merely on the basis of **X** | | | |

## Part III: Reverse Engineering & Context

*In this section, we move from abstract variables (X/Y) to specific content. You must "reverse engineer" the answer choice to determine what the stimulus must look like.*

**8. The Phrase:** *"Fails to consider that even if X, may not be Y"*

- **Logical Translation:** This describes a Flaw. The argument assumes that if you have **X** (Evidence), you generally get **Y** (Conclusion/Effect).
- **Write a Real-World Example:** Write a simple sentence using *raining* (X) and *getting wet* (Y) that fits this structure.
- *Your Argument:* _________________

**9. The Phrase:** *"Generalized too readily from the fruits it tested to the fruits it did not test"*

- **Reverse Engineer the Stimulus:** For this answer choice to be correct, what **two specific things** must exist in the text of the stimulus?
- *Requirement A (The Evidence):* _________________
- *Requirement B (The Conclusion):* _________________

**10. The Phrase:** *"Explaining a historical fact in terms of the artistic preferences of people"*

- **Logical Translation:** Remember, "Explanation" means Causation.
- **Identify the Cause and Effect:**
- *The Cause (The Explainer):* _________________
- *The Effect (The Result):* _________________

# Quiz 1 — TRANSLATION & CLUSTER SENTENCES

There will be a review quiz at the end of every chapter. These quizzes make sure you're absorbing the material in each chapter. If you don't know the answers to the quiz questions, there's more in the chapter for you to learn.

The quizzes are designed to be low-effort and require a basic minimum comprehension of the ideas discussed in the chapter. If you can't complete the quiz, you need to reread the chapter.

## Instructions

Answer these questions based on your knowledge of the chapter. Attempt to answer without looking back at the chapter first. If you don't know the answer, circle the question number and go find the answer in the chapter. Study the sections of the chapter that you couldn't remember at first.

## Word Bank

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| core(s) | optional element(s) | core element(s) |
| middle-out | sentence | specifier(s) |
| translation | studying | cluster sentence(s) |

Use the word bank to answer the fill-in-the-blank questions. Some words will not be used and others may be used more than once.

1. A(n) ________ contains at least one complete thought.
2. ________ are those little words that glue bigger words to explanations.
3. To tackle a cluster sentence with two or more commas, use ________.
4. ________ is the one skill you need to master Logical Reasoning.
5. **TRUE OR FALSE** — A core element is a piece of the sentence that can be removed without any bad grammatical consequences.
6. **TRUE OR FALSE** — Commas indicate that something on either side of the comma is a core element.
7. **TRUE OR FALSE** — The test writers purposefully write poorly.
8. **Seek and find!** Circle only the specifiers in the word cloud.

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| after | them | who | interrogatory |
| long | since | comma | ostentatious |
| when | cluster | for | that |

---

# Argument Parsing Worksheet

**Name:** ____________________

**Date:** ____________________

## Instructions

For each phrase below, identify the different parts of the argument as requested.

### Part 1: Conclusion & Evidence

*The Conclusion is the main point or claim. The Evidence is the reason or justification for the conclusion.*

1. **"Infers that X on the grounds that Y"** — Conclusion: ________ . Evidence: ________ .
2. **"Doing X by doing Y"** — Conclusion (the goal): ________ . Evidence (the method): ________ .
3. **"Using X to support Y"** — Conclusion: ________ . Evidence: ________ .
4. **"Concludes, from the claim that X, that Y"** — Conclusion: ________ . Evidence: ________ .

### Part 2: Premise & Conclusion

5. **"Concludes X on the basis of Y"** — Premise: ________ . Conclusion: ________ .
6. **"Infers, on the grounds that X, that Y"** — Premise: ________ . Conclusion: ________ .
7. **"Takes for granted that Y because X"** — Premise: ________ . Conclusion: ________ .
8. **"Y is true merely on the basis of X"** — Premise: ________ . Conclusion: ________ .

### Part 3: Bonus

9. **What is another name for a "Supported Premise?"** — ________________

// 5 "Why is this hard?" items for Module 1.
//
// Items are derived from the coachNote and trapRequirements fields of
// existing abstractChoices items. The student sees a stimulus snippet and an
// abstract answer choice, and picks what makes that choice hard to evaluate.
// Distractors are drawn from real LSAT trap categories (extreme language,
// scope shift, role confusion, missing match) so wrong picks are educational.
//
// No fresh LSAT stimuli authored here: every stimulus + answer choice is
// reframed from material already in data/abstractChoices.ts.

export interface WhyHardItem {
  id: string;
  stimulus: string;
  answerChoice: string;
  question: string;
  options: { text: string; correct: boolean; explanation: string }[];
}

  const distractors = METHOD_PATTERNS.filter((p) => p.id !== patternId)
    .slice((index * 3) % (METHOD_PATTERNS.length - 1), ((index * 3) % (METHOD_PATTERNS.length - 1)) + 3)
    .map((p) => p.plainEnglish);

  // Ensure 3 unique distractors. Fall back to top siblings if slice underran.
  while (distractors.length < 3) {
    const candidate = METHOD_PATTERNS.find(
      (p) => p.id !== patternId && !distractors.includes(p.plainEnglish),
    );
    if (!candidate) break;
    distractors.push(candidate.plainEnglish);
  }

  const options = [pattern.plainEnglish, ...distractors];
  // Deterministic shuffle by index so correctOption is reproducible
  const correctIndex = index % options.length;
  const reordered = [...options];
  const tmp = reordered[0];
  reordered[0] = reordered[correctIndex];
  reordered[correctIndex] = tmp;

  return {
    id: `mq_${patternId}`,
    stimulus: pattern.example.stimulus,
    prompt: "Which method of reasoning does this stimulus use?",
    options: reordered,
    correctOption: correctIndex,
    explanation: pattern.example.explanation,
  };
}

export const METHOD_QUIZ_ITEMS: MethodQuizItem[] = SELECTED_PATTERN_IDS.map((id, i) =>
  buildItem(id, i),
);
=====
// 10 "Spot the Trap" quiz items for Module 6.
//
// Each item presents a wrong-answer choice (lifted from a real drill in
// data/drills.ts: a wrong option whose explanation describes the trap), then
// asks the student to label the trap type. The four trap-type labels come
// directly from the trapTag taxonomy already documented at the top of
// data/drills.ts. The student picks from: wrong_role, wrong_method,
// structural_mismatch, attractive_language.
//
// No new LSAT content authored: every stimulus, every wrong-answer choice,
// and every explanation is a verbatim field from data/drills.ts.

import { DRILLS } from "./drills";
import { DrillItem } from "../types";

export type TrapTag =
  | "wrong_role"
  | "wrong_method"
  | "structural_mismatch"
  | "attractive_language";

export interface TrapItem {
  id: string;
  stimulus: string;
  wrongChoice: string;
  prompt: string;
  options: TrapTag[];
  correctOption: number;
  explanation: string;
}

const TRAP_LABELS: Record<TrapTag, string> = {
  wrong_role: "Wrong Role",
  wrong_method: "Wrong Method",
  structural_mismatch: "Structural Mismatch",
  attractive_language: "Attractive Language",
};

export const TRAP_OPTION_ORDER: TrapTag[] = [
  "wrong_role",
  "wrong_method",
  "structural_mismatch",
  "attractive_language",
];

export const TRAP_OPTION_TEXT: string[] = TRAP_OPTION_ORDER.map((tag) => TRAP_LABELS[tag]);

// Pick the first 10 drills that have a defined trapTag and at least one
// wrong option with a substantive explanation. Use option[0] when it is the
// wrong answer; otherwise use the first incorrect option.
function pickWrongOption(drill: DrillItem): { text: string; explanation: string } | null {
  for (let i = 0; i < drill.options.length; i++) {
    if (i === drill.correctOptionIndex) continue;
    const opt = drill.options[i];
    if (opt.explanation && opt.explanation.length > 20) return opt;
  }
  return null;
}

const SOURCE_DRILLS = DRILLS.filter((d) => Boolean(d.trapTag)).slice(0, 10);

export const TRAP_ITEMS: TrapItem[] = SOURCE_DRILLS.map((drill, index) => {
  const wrong = pickWrongOption(drill);
  const trapTag = (drill.trapTag as TrapTag) ?? "attractive_language";
  return {
    id: `trap_${drill.id}`,
    stimulus: drill.stimulus ?? "",
    wrongChoice: wrong?.text ?? drill.options[0].text,
    prompt: "What kind of trap is this wrong answer?",
    options: TRAP_OPTION_ORDER,
    correctOption: TRAP_OPTION_ORDER.indexOf(trapTag),
    explanation: wrong?.explanation ?? drill.explanation,
  };
});

drill_1 [role/wrong_role] key=1
  0  It is the argument's main conclusion.
      -> The main conclusion is "The new highway project will be completed ahead of schedule." It is what the author is trying to prove.
  1* It is a premise offered in support of the argument's conclusion.
      -> Correct. The claim about double shifts directly supports the conclusion that the project finished early.
  2  It is an intermediate conclusion that supports the main conclusion.
      -> An intermediate conclusion requires its own supporting evidence. The shift claim is simply assertive evidence here.
  3  It is a concession to a potential counterargument.
      -> A concession would acknowledge an opposing point of view (e.g., "Although the work was hard..."). This claim provides direct justification.

drill_2 [method/wrong_method] key=1
  0  Providing an alternative explanation for the recent outage.
      -> The IT Lead does not explain why the outage happened; they provide counter-evidence to the suggestion to switch.
  1* Pointing out that the opponent failed to take relevant information into account.
      -> Correct. The IT Lead introduces server performance data (5-year uptime, the other provider's failures) that the Manager completely ignored.
  2  Deriving a general principle from specific examples.
      -> The IT Lead is not trying to find a rule here, they are comparing performance records.
  3  Showing that the opponent's conclusion leads to an impossible result.
      -> The IT Lead does not show that switching is logically impossible; they argue it is a bad idea given the data.

drill_3 [flaw/structural_mismatch] key=1
  0  Mistakes a temporal relationship between two events for a causal relationship.
      -> The argument is not about the order of events; it is about logical requirements.
  1* Presumes, without providing justification, that a condition necessary for an outcome is also sufficient to produce that outcome.
      -> Correct. The argument mistakes necessary (must pass test) for sufficient (if she passed test, she must be allowed).
  2  Relies on an inappropriate analogy between passing a test and legal permission.
      -> There is no analogy here; it is a direct, flawed deductive inference.
  3  Attacks the person making a claim rather than the claim itself.
      -> There is no personal attack here, only a structural flaw in the reasoning.

drill_4 [one_word_trap/attractive_language] key=1
  0  It is a premise that completely proves the main conclusion that reading fiction is not a waste of time.
      -> Wrong. Premises support a conclusion; they rarely completely prove it in a formal sense. This is an overstatement.
  1* It is a premise offered to counter the claim that reading fiction is a waste of time.
      -> Correct. The claim builds empathy, which the author uses to argue against the idea that fiction is a waste of time.

drill_5 [role/wrong_role] key=1
  0  It is the main conclusion of the argument.
      -> The main conclusion is the final sentence ("we should expand, not cut, public arts funding").
  1* It is an intermediate conclusion supported by the dollar-multiplier claim and used to support the main conclusion.
      -> Correct. The dollar-multiplier evidence supports the city-economy claim, which supports the main conclusion.
  2  It is a counterconsideration the argument acknowledges before rejecting.
      -> The author is not acknowledging an opposing point here; they are advancing their own evidence chain.
  3  It is a premise that no other statement supports.
      -> The previous sentence ("every dollar generates three dollars") supports this claim, so it is not an unsupported premise.

drill_6 [role/wrong_role] key=0
  0* It is background information that sets context but does not support the main conclusion.
      -> Correct. "Although" signals a concession or background framing. The flooding worry is not used as evidence.
  1  It is the main conclusion of the argument.
      -> The...

import { readFileSync, writeFileSync } from "node:fs";
const file = "C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-abstract/src/topics/abstract/data/drills.ts";
const { DRILLS } = await import("file:///" + file);
const src = readFileSync(file, "utf8");
const header = src.slice(0, src.indexOf("export const DRILLS"));
const target: Record<string, number> = {
  drill_3: 2, drill_5: 3, drill_6: 2, drill_7: 1, drill_8: 3, drill_9: 2, drill_10: 0,
  drill_11: 3, drill_12: 1, drill_13: 2, drill_14: 3, drill_15: 1, drill_16: 2, drill_17: 3,
  drill_4: 0, drill_18: 1, drill_19: 1, drill_20: 0,
};
const strip = (t: string) => t
  .replace(/^This is method \d+ from the 17 Methods source: /, "")
  .replace(/ ?Method \d+ from the 17 Methods source\.?/g, "")
  .replace(/^providing/, "Providing");
for (const d of DRILLS) {
  if (d.id === "drill_1") {
    d.options[1].explanation = "Correct. The double shifts are a reason the author gives for the conclusion that the project will finish ahead of schedule.";
    d.options[2].explanation = "An intermediate conclusion has support of its own. Nothing in the argument supports the claim about double shifts, so it is a plain premise.";
  }
  d.explanation = strip(d.explanation);
  d.options.forEach((o: any) => (o.explanation = strip(o.explanation)));
  const t = target[d.id];
  if (t !== undefined && t !== d.correctOptionIndex) {
    const opts = [...d.options];
    [opts[t], opts[d.correctOptionIndex]] = [opts[d.correctOptionIndex], opts[t]];
    d.options = opts;
    d.correctOptionIndex = t;
  }
}
const q = (s: string) => JSON.stringify(s);
const out = DRILLS.map((d: any) => `  {
    id: ${q(d.id)},
    type: ${q(d.type)},
    stimulus:
      ${q(d.stimulus)},
    question: ${q(d.question)},
    options: [
${d.options.map((o: any) => `      { text: ${q(o.text)}, explanation: ${q(o.explanation)} },`).join("\n")}
    ],
    correctOptionIndex: ${d.correctOptionIndex},
    explanation:
      ${q(d.explanation)},${d.trapTag ? `\n    trapTag: ${q(d.trapTag)},` : ""}
  },`).join("\n");
const newHeader = header.replace("// 20 drill items distributed as 5 role, 5 method, 5 flaw, 5 one_word_trap.",
  "// 20 drill items: 5 role, 6 method, 5 flaw, 4 one_word_trap.\n//\n// Correct answers are spread across choice positions. drill_1 and drill_2 keep\n// their original order because saved attempts point at those positions.");
writeFileSync(file, newHeader + "export const DRILLS: DrillItem[] = [\n" + out + "\n];\n");
const counts = [0,0,0,0]; DRILLS.forEach((d: any) => counts[d.correctOptionIndex]++);
console.log("key positions", counts);
EOF

@media (min-width: 768px) {
  .topic-abstract .lesson-cased p {

  }
}

/* Demote any inline text that is shouting in the legacy lesson markup:
 * any element sized above text-base AND wearing uppercase or italic
 * loses those treatments. text-xs and text-[10px] labels keep theirs. */
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-lg,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-xl,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-2xl,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-3xl,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-4xl,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-5xl,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased .text-6xl {
  text-transform: none;
  font-style: normal;
}

/* Body paragraphs in legacy LessonReader markup are typically tagged
 * font-bold; demote to normal weight here. font-black remains on
 * actual headers (sized above text-base) where the cn() chain still
 * applies black. */
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased p.font-bold,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased p .font-bold {
  font-weight: 400;
}

/* Strong + em inside paragraphs keep their semantics in cased form. */
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased p strong {
  font-weight: 700;
}

.topic-abstract .lesson-cased p em {
  font-style: italic;
  font-weight: inherit;
}

.topic-abstract .lesson-cased ul,
.topic-abstract .lesson-cased ol {
  font-size: 1rem;
  line-height: 1.7;
  margin-bottom: 1.25rem;
  padding-left: 1.5rem;
}

.topic-abstract .lesson-cased li {
  margin-bottom: 0.5rem;
  font-weight: 400;
}

command: S=/c/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/e73e83bf-cdd9-4e5c-a7ed-0a7661973760/scratchpad; cat > $S/kw.tsx <<'EOF'
// Clickable words for the keyword step. Real buttons, so they work from the
// keyboard and announce their pressed state.
function WordPicker({
  tokens,
  picked,
  onToggle,
  locked,
  isCorrectWord,
}: {
  tokens: string[];
  picked: Set<number>;
  onToggle: (i: number) => void;
  locked: boolean;
  isCorrectWord: (token: string) => boolean;
}) {
  return (
    <p className="text-lg md:text-xl font-semibold leading-loose m-0">
      {tokens.map((t, i) => {
        if (!/\w/.test(t)) return <span key={i}>{t}</span>;
        const isPicked = picked.has(i);
        const right = locked && isCorrectWord(t);
        return (
          <button
            key={i}
            type="button"
            disabled={locked}
            aria-pressed={isPicked}
            onClick={() => onToggle(i)}
            className={cn(
              "px-0.5 transition-colors",
              !locked && isPicked && "bg-brutal-yellow outline outline-2 outline-brutal-ink",
              !locked && !isPicked && "hover:bg-brutal-yellow/40",
              locked && right && isPicked && "bg-brutal-green",
              locked && right && !isPicked && "bg-brutal-yellow underline decoration-2",
              locked && isPicked && !right && "bg-brutal-red text-brutal-paper line-through",
            )}
          >
            {t}
          </button>
        );
      })}
    </p>
  );
}

// Keyword extraction (Module 3 quiz): click the keywords, write the plain
// English version, then compare it with the model sentence. Keywords are
// auto-graded. The sentence is a self-check: "not quite" turns a correct
// keyword score into partial credit.
function KeywordExtractionRenderer({
  item,
  onAnswer,
  onNext,
}: {
  item: typeof TRANSLATOR_QUIZ_ITEMS[number];
  onAnswer: (verdict: boolean | QuizVerdict) => void;
  onNext: () => void;
}) {
  const { markTranslatorComplete } = useApp();
  const tokens = useMemo(() => splitTokens(item.stemText), [item.stemText]);
  const [picked, setPicked] = useState<Set<number>>(new Set());
  const [translation, setTranslation] = useState("");
  const [submitted, setSubmitted] = useState(false);
  const [finalVerdict, setFinalVerdict] = useState<QuizVerdict | null>(null);
  const position = TRANSLATOR_QUIZ_ITEMS.findIndex((i) => i.stemNumber === item.stemNumber) + 1;

  const correctLower = item.correctKeywords.map((k) => k.toLowerCase());
  const isCorrectWord = (t: string) => correctLower.includes(stripPunc(t).toLowerCase());

  const togglePick = (i: number) => {
    setPicked((prev) => {
      const next = new Set(prev);
      if (next.has(i)) next.delete(i);
      else next.add(i);
      return next;
    });
  };

  const canSubmit = picked.size > 0 && translation.trim().length >= 10;
  const pickedWords = Array.from(picked).map((i) => stripPunc(tokens[i]).toLowerCase());
  const matched = correctLower.filter((k) => pickedWords.includes(k));
  const wrong = pickedWords.filter((w) => !correctLower.includes(w));
  const pctCorrect = correctLower.length > 0 ? matched.length / correctLower.length : 0;
  const keywordVerdict: QuizVerdict =
    pctCorrect >= 0.75 && wrong.length === 0
      ? "correct"
      : pctCorrect >= 0.75 && wrong.length <= 2
        ? "partial"
        : "incorrect";

  const submit = () => {
    setSubmitted(true);
    void markTranslatorComplete(
      item.stemNumber,
      Array.from(picked).map((i) => tokens[i]),
      translation,
    );
  };

  const selfCheck = (same: boolean) => {
    const verdict: QuizVerdict = !same && keywordVerdict === "correct" ? "partial" : keywordVerdict;
    setFinalVerdict(verdict);
    onAnswer(verdict);
  };

  return (
    <div className="bg-brutal-paper border-4 border-brutal-ink p-5 md:p-6 brutal-shadow">
      <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-3">
        Answer choice {position} of {TRANSLATOR_QUIZ_ITEMS.length}
      </p>
      <p className="font-black mb-2">Step 1. Click the words that carry the meaning.</p>
      <div className="bg-white border-4 border-brutal-ink p-4 md:p-5 mb-5">
        <WordPicker
          tokens={tokens}
          picked={picked}
          onToggle={togglePick}
          locked={submitted}
          isCorrectWord={isCorrectWord}
        />
      </div>
      <p className="font-black mb-2">Step 2. Say it in plain English.</p>
      <textarea
        value={translation}
        onChange={(e) => setTranslation(e.target.value)}
        disabled={submitted}
        aria-label="Plain English version"
        placeholder="The statement is..."
        className="w-full min-h-[96px] border-2 border-brutal-ink p-3 font-medium bg-white"
      />
      {!submitted && (
        <button
          type="button"
          disabled={!canSubmit}
          onClick={submit}
          className={cn("mt-4 brutal-btn", canSubmit ? "bg-brutal-yellow" : "bg-brutal-paper opacity-60")}
        >
          Check my work
        </button>
      )}
      {submitted && (
        <div className="mt-5 space-y-4">
          <p className="font-medium">
            <span className="font-black">Keywords:</span> you found {matched.length} of {correctLower.length}.{" "}
            {wrong.length > 0
              ? `${wrong.length} of your picks ${wrong.length === 1 ? "is" : "are"} filler (struck through). Green is a keyword you found; yellow is one you missed.`
              : "No filler picked."}
          </p>
          <div className="grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-3">
            <div className="bg-white border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
              <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Yours</p>
              <p className="font-medium m-0">{translation}</p>
            </div>
            <div className="bg-brutal-cyan/30 border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
              <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Model</p>
              <p className="font-medium m-0">{item.idealTranslation}</p>
            </div>
          </div>
          {finalVerdict === null ? (
            <div>
              <p className="font-black mb-2">Self-check: does yours say the same thing as the model?</p>
              <div className="flex flex-wrap gap-3">
                <button type="button" onClick={() => selfCheck(true)} className="brutal-btn bg-brutal-green">
                  Yes, same idea
                </button>
                <button type="button" onClick={() => selfCheck(false)} className="brutal-btn bg-white">
                  Not quite
                </button>
              </div>
            </div>
          ) : (
            <>
              <VerdictHeader verdict={finalVerdict} />
              <div className="bg-white border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
                <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Walkthrough</p>
                {item.walkthrough.reasoning.map((p, i) => (
                  <p key={i} className="font-medium mb-2">
                    <TermText>{p}</TermText>
                  </p>
                ))}
              </div>
              {item.walkthrough.vocab.length > 0 && (
                <div className="grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-2">
                  {item.walkthrough.vocab.map((v, i) => (
                    <div key={i} className="bg-brutal-yellow border-2 border-brutal-ink p-3">
                      <p className="font-black text-sm">{v.term}</p>
                      <p className="font-medium m-0">{v.meaning}</p>
                      {v.note && <p className="text-sm mt-1 m-0">{v.note}</p>}
                    </div>
                  ))}
                </div>
              )}
              {item.walkthrough.requirements.length > 0 && (
                <div className="bg-brutal-cyan/30 border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
                  <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">The stimulus must have</p>
                  <ul className="list-disc pl-5 space-y-1 m-0">
                    {item.walkthrough.requirements.map((r, i) => (
                      <li key={i} className="font-medium">
                        {r}
                      </li>
                    ))}
                  </ul>
                </div>
              )}
              <div className="bg-white border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
                <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Worked example</p>
                <p className="italic font-medium mb-2">{item.walkthrough.example.stimulus}</p>
                <p className="font-medium text-brutal-ink-soft m-0">{item.walkthrough.example.explanation}</p>
              </div>
              <button type="button" onClick={onNext} className="brutal-btn bg-brutal-green inline-flex items-center gap-2">
                Next answer choice <ArrowRight className="w-4 h-4" />
              </button>
            </>
          )}
        </div>
      )}
    </div>
  );
}

EOF
cat > $S/gauntlet.tsx <<'EOF'
// Decoding Gauntlet (Module 7 quiz): keywords, plain English, then pick what
// the stimulus must contain. Requirements and trap descriptions are mixed in
// a stable shuffled order; the student checks only the real requirements.
function seededShuffle<T>(items: T[], seed: number): T[] {
  let h = seed * 2654435761;
  const out = [...items];
  for (let i = out.length - 1; i > 0; i--) {
    h = (h * 1103515245 + 12345) >>> 0;
    const j = h % (i + 1);
    [out[i], out[j]] = [out[j], out[i]];
  }
  return out;
}

function DecodingGauntletRenderer({
  item,
  onAnswer,
  onNext,
}: {
  item: typeof GAUNTLET_QUIZ_ITEMS[number];
  onAnswer: (verdict: boolean | QuizVerdict) => void;
  onNext: () => void;
}) {
  const { recordGauntletItem } = useApp();
  const tokens = useMemo(() => splitTokens(item.original), [item.original]);
  const correctLower = item.correctKeywords.map((k) => k.toLowerCase());
  const isCorrectWord = (t: string) =>
    correctLower.some((k) => k.split(/\s+/).includes(stripPunc(t).toLowerCase()));
  const allRequirements = useMemo(
    () => [...item.stimulusRequirements, ...item.trapRequirements].map((text, index) => ({ text, index })),
    [item],
  );
  const shuffled = useMemo(() => seededShuffle(allRequirements, item.id), [allRequirements, item.id]);
  const isRequired = (index: number) => index < item.stimulusRequirements.length;
  const position = GAUNTLET_QUIZ_ITEMS.findIndex((c) => c.id === item.id) + 1;

  const [picked, setPicked] = useState<Set<number>>(new Set());
  const [translation, setTranslation] = useState("");
  const [reqChecked, setReqChecked] = useState<Set<number>>(new Set());
  const [submitted, setSubmitted] = useState(false);

  const pickedWords = Array.from(picked).map((i) => stripPunc(tokens[i]).toLowerCase());
  const wrong = pickedWords.filter((w) => !correctLower.some((k) => k.split(/\s+/).includes(w)));
  const phrasesHit = correctLower.filter((k) => k.split(/\s+/).some((part) => pickedWords.includes(part))).length;
  const keywordsOk = correctLower.length > 0 && phrasesHit / correctLower.length >= 0.75 && wrong.length <= 2;
  const reqsOk = allRequirements.every((r) => reqChecked.has(r.index) === isRequired(r.index));
  const verdict: QuizVerdict =
    keywordsOk && reqsOk && wrong.length === 0 ? "correct" : keywordsOk || reqsOk ? "partial" : "incorrect";

  const toggle = (setter: typeof setPicked, i: number) =>
    setter((prev) => {
      const next = new Set(prev);
      if (next.has(i)) next.delete(i);
      else next.add(i);
      return next;
    });

  const submit = () => {
    setSubmitted(true);
    onAnswer(verdict);
    void recordGauntletItem({
      choiceId: item.id,
      keywordsPicked: Array.from(picked).map((i) => tokens[i]),
      translationText: translation,
      requirementsChecked: Array.from(reqChecked),
      correctCount: phrasesHit,
    });
  };

  return (
    <div className="bg-brutal-paper border-4 border-brutal-ink p-5 md:p-6 brutal-shadow">
      <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-3">
        Answer choice {position} of {GAUNTLET_QUIZ_ITEMS.length}
      </p>
      <p className="font-black mb-2">Step 1. Click the words that carry the meaning.</p>
      <div className="bg-white border-4 border-brutal-ink p-4 md:p-5 mb-5">
        <WordPicker
          tokens={tokens}
          picked={picked}
          onToggle={(i) => toggle(setPicked, i)}
          locked={submitted}
          isCorrectWord={isCorrectWord}
        />
      </div>
      <p className="font-black mb-2">Step 2. Say it in plain English.</p>
      <textarea
        value={translation}
        onChange={(e) => setTranslation(e.target.value)}
        disabled={submitted}
        aria-label="Plain English version"
        placeholder="The statement is..."
        className="w-full min-h-[80px] border-2 border-brutal-ink p-3 font-medium bg-white mb-5"
      />
      <p className="font-black mb-2">Step 3. Which of these must be true of the stimulus for this choice to be right?</p>
      <p className="text-sm font-medium text-brutal-ink-soft mb-3">Pick every one that applies. Some describe ways the choice would be wrong.</p>
      <div className="space-y-2 mb-5">
        {shuffled.map((r) => {
          const checked = reqChecked.has(r.index);
          const required = isRequired(r.index);
          const rowRight = checked === required;
          return (
            <button
              key={r.index}
              type="button"
              disabled={submitted}
              aria-pressed={checked}
              onClick={() => toggle(setReqChecked, r.index)}
              className={cn(
                "w-full text-left flex items-start gap-3 border-2 border-brutal-ink p-3 font-medium transition-colors",
                !submitted && (checked ? "bg-brutal-cyan/50" : "bg-white hover:bg-brutal-cyan/15"),
                submitted && required && "bg-brutal-green/50",
                submitted && !required && checked && "bg-brutal-red/20",
                submitted && !required && !checked && "bg-white opacity-70",
              )}
            >
              <span
                className={cn(
                  "w-5 h-5 mt-0.5 border-2 border-brutal-ink shrink-0 flex items-center justify-center",
                  checked ? "bg-brutal-ink text-brutal-paper" : "bg-white",
                )}
                aria-hidden
              >
                {checked && <CheckCircle2 className="w-3.5 h-3.5" />}
              </span>
              <span className="flex-1">
                {r.text}
                {submitted && (
                  <span className="block text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-wider mt-1">
                    {required
                      ? checked
                        ? "Required. You caught it."
                        : "Required. You missed it."
                      : checked
                        ? "Not required. This would make the choice wrong."
                        : "Correctly left out."}
                    {!rowRight && " "}
                  </span>
                )}
              </span>
            </button>
          );
        })}
      </div>
      {!submitted ? (
        <button
          type="button"
          disabled={picked.size === 0 || translation.trim().length < 10 || reqChecked.size === 0}
          onClick={submit}
          className="brutal-btn bg-brutal-yellow disabled:opacity-60"
        >
          Check my work
        </button>
      ) : (
        <div className="space-y-3">
          <VerdictHeader verdict={verdict} />
          <p className="text-sm font-medium">
            Keywords: {phrasesHit} of {correctLower.length}. Stimulus checklist: {reqsOk ? "all right" : "not all right"}.
          </p>
          <div className="grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-3">
            <div className="bg-white border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
              <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Yours</p>
              <p className="font-medium m-0">{translation}</p>
            </div>
            <div className="bg-brutal-cyan/30 border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
              <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Model</p>
              <p className="font-medium m-0">{item.plainEnglish}</p>
            </div>
          </div>
          <div className="bg-brutal-yellow border-2 border-brutal-ink p-4">
            <p className="text-xs font-black uppercase tracking-widest mb-2">Coach note</p>
            <p className="font-medium m-0">{item.coachNote}</p>
          </div>
          <button type="button" onClick={onNext} className="brutal-btn bg-brutal-green inline-flex items-center gap-2">
            Next <ArrowRight className="w-4 h-4" />
          </button>
        </div>
      )}
    </div>
  );
}

description: Make brutal palette support opacity modifiers
command: cd /c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-abstract && python - <<'EOF'
import re
p='tailwind.config.ts'
s=open(p,encoding='utf-8').read()
old_comment='''         * (src/topics/abstract/styles/brutal.css). Hex via var() because the
         * brutalist palette is intentionally non-themed. */'''
assert old_comment in s
s=s.replace(old_comment,'''         * (src/topics/abstract/styles/brutal.css). The vars hold oklch()
         * colors, so each token goes through color-mix with <alpha-value>:
         * a bare var() silently dropped opacity shorthands like
         * bg-brutal-green/40 (no CSS was generated for them). */''')
for name in ["yellow","cyan","pink","green","red","ink","ink-soft","ink-faint","ink-trace","bg","paper"]:
    a=f'"brutal-{name}": "var(--brutal-{name})",'
    assert a in s, a
    s=s.replace(a,f'"brutal-{name}": "color-mix(in oklch, var(--brutal-{name}) calc(<alpha-value> * 100%), transparent)",')
open(p,'w',encoding='utf-8',newline='\n').write(s)
EOF

## assistant

I've rebuilt the Abstract bootcamp locally. Nothing is committed or live yet, and lsatprep.study is unchanged.

**What was wrong with the structure**
- **The breaking-down method was taught in the wrong place.** Module 1 taught the full method with a worked example. Module 3, which is supposed to teach it, was about 100 words with no example. The method also appeared in four different versions across the modules.
- **No drill asked for any breaking down.** The 20 drills were handed out to modules in numeric order, whatever their topic, so "Spot the Method" got two Flaw questions. Nothing asked students to go from abstract to concrete or back, even though your parsing worksheets do both.
- **Notes and quizzes didn't match.** The Module 2 quiz tested words the note never taught. The Module 5 note repeated the vocabulary instead of teaching methods. The Module 6 quiz used four trap labels that no note defined.
- **The same 14 answer choices appeared three times**, in the Module 3 quiz, the Module 7 note and the Module 7 quiz.
- **Some grading was broken.** The written translations were never checked. The trap quiz graded against the wrong label. Every drill answer was choice 1 or 2.
- **Every module after the first was locked**, including on your account.

**What I changed**
- **Module 3 is now "Break It Down" and holds the method in one place.** The note walks through it in four steps, and the student does each one on a real answer choice, ending with checking it against two stimuli (one fits, one is missing a piece).
- **New 12-item drill for Module 3, built from your worksheets:**
  - Find the direction words and say which letter is the conclusion.
  - Decode an abstract answer choice, pick the argument it describes, then write one of your own and compare it with a model.
  - Read a concrete argument and build its abstract description.
  - A wrong pick shows why, and the student tries again.
- **All seven notes rewritten** so each note matches its quiz. All 20 drills are now assigned by topic, and no answer choice is repeated between quizzes.
- **Grading fixed:** translations are compared with a model and self-checked, Module 7 has a "what must the stimulus contain" step, trap labels are correct per answer, and the answer key is spread across all four positions. I left the answer order of drills 1 and 2 alone, because your two test attempts point at those positions. No student has any Abstract progress, so nothing was stranded.
- **Everything is unlocked.** Module names no longer get cut off, and the Library card now lists the modules.
- **A display bug that had hidden all the green and red feedback boxes is fixed.** The bootcamp's colours didn't support the faded-tint style the boxes use, so those boxes never showed a colour. That affected the old pages too.

**How I checked it:** In your Chrome I went through the Module 3 note demo, the full drill, the Module 3 and Module 7 quizzes, and the Module 1 note, with no console errors. Typecheck passes and lint has no errors. Two tests in an unrelated file (`DrillLaunchGate`) fail; that file imports nothing I changed.

**Not checked yet:** saving progress while signed in (the local preview has no user, so nothing saves), the teacher Library card on screen, phone widths, clicking through the notes for Modules 2, 4, 5 and 6, and a production build.

The preview is open in your Chrome at `localhost:8080/dev/abstract-preview`. That page only exists on your local dev server.

**Decisions for you:**
1. **Ship it?** Say the word and I'll commit, open a PR, merge, and publish with the free deploy (no Lovable credits).
2. Keep the yellow look, or move Abstract to the same lesson look as Intro to LR and Conditionals?
3. Rename "Abstract" in the Library to something like "Abstract Answer Choices"?

The full audit and build log are in [brainstorms/2026-10-12-abstract-bootcamp-fix.md](brainstorms/2026-10-12-abstract-bootcamp-fix.md).

ship it

Rebuild the Abstract bootcamp around breaking down answer choices

The method was taught in Module 1 while Module 3, named the core
method, was a short list with no example. Drills were sliced 1-20 in
order across modules regardless of topic, notes and quizzes taught and
tested different terms, the same 14 answer choices appeared three
times, and every module after the first was locked.

- Unlock every module, note, quiz and drill.
- Rewrite all seven notes (AbstractNotes.tsx); teach the method once,
  in Module 3 "Break It Down", with a student-driven demo.
- New 12-item Break It Down drill: X/Y direction words, decode then
  make it concrete, and build the abstract description.
- Remap drills by topic; spread the answer key; strip internal
  "17 Methods source" labels; fix drill_1's explanation.
- Quizzes: Module 1 "which sentence?", Module 2 tests what the note
  teaches, Module 3 translation self-check, Module 6 per-choice trap
  labels, Module 7 stimulus checklist on unseen choices.
- Brutal palette tokens go through color-mix so opacity shorthands
  render (tinted feedback boxes were invisible).
- Wrap module names, Library card outline, accurate progress totals,
  dev-only /dev/abstract-preview route.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
EOF

## Why
Audit of the Abstract bootcamp (2026-10-12): the breakdown method was taught in Module 1 while Module 3 was a bare list; drills were sliced 1-20 across modules regardless of topic; notes and quizzes taught and tested different terms; the same 14 answer choices appeared three times; translations were never checked; trap quiz graded against the wrong label; every drill key was choice 1 or 2; modules 2-7 were locked.

## What
- Everything unlocked; progress still tracked.
- Seven notes rewritten (`AbstractNotes.tsx`); the method is taught once, in Module 3 "Break It Down", with a student-driven demo.
- New 12-item Break It Down drill (X/Y direction words, decode then make it concrete, build the abstract description), from Joshua's parsing worksheets.
- Drills remapped by topic, key spread across positions (drill_1/2 untouched: saved attempts), internal source labels removed.
- Quiz fixes for Modules 1, 2, 3, 6, 7; no answer choice repeats between quizzes.
- `brutal-*` Tailwind tokens wrapped in `color-mix` so opacity shorthands render (tinted feedback boxes were invisible).
- Module names wrap, Library card outline, progress totals count reachable items only, dev-only `/dev/abstract-preview`.

No student has Abstract progress (checked in the DB), so no work is stranded.

## Verification
- `tsc` clean, `npm run build` passes, lint 0 errors on changed files.
- Walked in Chrome on the dev preview: Module 3 note demo, full drill, Module 3 and 7 quizzes, Module 1 note. No console errors.
- Not verified: signed-in persistence, teacher Library card, mobile widths.
- `DrillLaunchGate.test.tsx` has 2 failures; it imports nothing in this change.

## assistant

[external_agent_tool_result]
Pushing to `main` makes Lovable build the commit — its project record shows a
preview screenshot tagged with the commit sha within minutes — but the published
site at lsatprep.study keeps serving the previous bundle until something calls
`deploy_project` (Lovable MCP, `project_id 53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76`,
name `lsatu`). Confirmed 2026-09-08: a fix sat built-but-unpublished for 20
minutes with no error anywhere.

**The sync lags the push.** `deploy_project` publishes whatever Lovable has
ingested, not what is on origin. On 2026-09-09 a deploy fired seconds after a
successful push and shipped the *previous* commit; `get_project` showed
`latest_commit_sha` catching up about three minutes later, and a second deploy
was needed. There is no error and no warning — the deploy reports `pending` and
succeeds, just on the wrong commit.

**Why:** build, sync and publish are three separate steps, and none of them
reports that the others have not happened. A green push reads as shipped.

**How to apply:** before calling `deploy_project`, call `get_project` and check
`latest_commit_sha` equals your HEAD; if it does not, wait and re-check. After
deploying, prove it from the bundle: pick a string **the commit alone**
introduces — not a class name an earlier deploy already shipped, which is how a
stale bundle passed a check on 2026-09-09 — then fetch `https://lsatprep.study/`,
read the `index-*` asset hash out of it, and grep the asset for the marker.
[/external_agent_tool_result]

## assistant

Both drill issues are fixed and live on lsatprep.study. I confirmed it by finding the new drill 20 text in the code the live site is serving.

- **Four choices on every drill.** Drills 4, 18, 19 and 20 had only two choices, so a guess was a coin flip. Each now has four, and every wrong choice explains why it's wrong.
- **Drill 20 has its own story.** It no longer reuses drill 9's traffic camera. The new one: a school board credits its homework policy for higher reading scores, but it was also the first year every classroom had a reading aide. The skill tested is the same: the near miss says "conclusively proves," which is too strong.
- **Drill 4's explanation fixed.** It said "first option" and "second option," but those positions had changed when I spread out the answer key last round. The explanation no longer refers to positions.

The answer key is still spread across all four positions, and the typecheck passes.

The first deploy went out before Lovable had finished building the new commit, so the site briefly still showed the old drills. After a couple of minutes the site was serving the new version and drill 20's new text was live.

**Shipped:** [vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db#209](https://github.com/vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db/pull/209), merged as `fcdc32b8` and published with the free deploy (no Lovable credits).

Still open: whether to keep the yellow look, and whether to rename "Abstract" in the Library. The stray `supabase/functions/mcp/index.ts` change is still left uncommitted.
